- From level 2 node J, there is a direct edge J–I. That gives path L → M → J → I, also length 3.
- Verify no shorter path exists: L is not directly connected to I; none of L’s level-1 neighbors (E or M) connect directly to I.

Therefore, the shortest length is 3. One valid shortest path is L → E → P → I.

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are N and H.
- List neighbors:
  - N connects to Y, C, B.
  - H connects to B and G (and the big outer frame from G continues back, but H’s only incident edges are (H, B) and (H, G)).

All N→H paths must first leave N via one of {N–Y, N–C, N–B} and must enter H via one of {B–H, G–H}. Therefore, to disconnect N from H it suffices to cut both edges incident to H, because no path can reach H without using either (B, H) or (G, H).

Verification:
- Remove (B, H). Any path must then use (G, H) to reach H. Is there a path from N to G? Yes: N–B–E–F–X–K–G, or N–C–I–P–J–…– outer frame to G, etc. So one cut is not enough.
- Remove both (B, H) and (G, H). Then H has degree 0 and is isolated. Every possible path from N to H is broken because H has no remaining incident edges.

Minimality:
- Any cut must include at least one of H’s two incident edges; otherwise H remains reachable via that edge. With only one removed, a path via the other remains. Hence at least 2 edges are necessary and sufficient.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']]}

Image name: SEIS_task2_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- The two black nodes are E (left) and I (center-right).
- Enumerate all E-to-I paths by following visible continuous lines:

1) Upper routes via G/H:
   - E–G–H–I
   - E–G–F–X–K–J–I
   - E–G–F–K–J–I
   - E–G–X–K–J–I
   - E–G–K–J–I
   - E–M–J–I
   - E–M–J–P–I
   - E–M–J–D–(back to J only; no direct to I except through J/P), so these all funnel through either H or J (or P via J).

2) Middle routes through C/O/P:
   - E–B–C–O–P–I
   - E–N–C–O–P–I
   - E–N–O–P–I
   - E–B–C–H–I
   - E–N–C–H–I
   - E–N–B–C–… (same funnels)

3) Any route from E down-left must go via B or N first; from there to reach I without using H must go through O→P or J (since O connects to P and J connects to I or P). From E upward (via M or G/F/X/K), to reach I without H, they all go through J (or J→P→I).

- Therefore every E–I path must pass through at least one of the nodes in the set {H, J, P}.
   - Paths using the G–H–I link use H.
   - Paths that avoid H go to the right via J (e.g., through M/G/F/X/K to J) or through O→P (from below) which then reaches I; P also connects to J, but still P is the last step to I on those lower routes.
- Check minimality:
   - Removing a single node is insufficient:
     • Remove H: paths remain via E–M–J–I or E–B–C–O–P–I.
     • Remove J: paths remain via E–G–H–I or via E–B/N–C–H–I.
     • Remove P: paths remain via E–M–J–I or E–G–…–J–I; and via E–G–H–I.
   - Try size-2 cut:
     • Remove H and J: lower routes via O→P→I still exist (E–B/N–…–O–P–I). So not sufficient.
     • Remove H and P: upper routes via J remain (E–M–J–I). Not sufficient.
     • Remove J and P: all routes that avoid H are blocked; remaining potential routes must use H, but J and P are irrelevant then. Paths via H remain (E–G–H–I). So not sufficient.
   - Size-3 cut {H, J, P}:
     • Any path that would use H is blocked.
     • Any path that would avoid H must go through J or through P; both removed.
     • Thus all E–I paths are cut.

- No smaller cut works (as shown), so the minimum is 3.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, J, P]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'M']}
Progress: 5/280 images processed.

- We showed any 1-node cut fails.

No other path remains from J to the graph leading to F.

- If only (H,I) is cut, there is G–F–K–J–I or G–X–K–J–I.

- Remove (J, I) to block every route from the upper-right side (via E/F/X/K → J → I).

Cutting P–I alone is insufficient because G–H–C–I would remain; cutting C–I alone is insufficient because G–H–P–I would remain.

Therefore, a minimal cut of size 2 is removing (H, I) plus either (P, I) or (C, I) or (J, I) depending on which side we block. The combination that severs all remaining paths with two edges is:
- Remove (H, I) and (J, I).

- Through H: blocked by (H, I).
- Through J: blocked by (J, I).

- Remove (H, I) and (C, I).

- Direct path G–H–I: broken by (H, I).

From G to J exists without H (e.g., G–E–M–J). From G to C or P must pass through H (edges H–C and H–P exist; there are no other routes from G’s side to C or P). Therefore:

However, if instead of cutting (J,I), we cut both (C,I) and (P,I) along with (H,I) would be three. But can we do size 2 by a different strategy? Cut at G: remove two edges from G that are mandatory? G has four disjoint options (E,F,X,H), and from each there is a route to I unless we also cut elsewhere; thus need more than 2 around G.

Consider cut set of size 2: remove (G,E) and (G,H)? Then G could go via F or X to K–J–I. So not enough.

Try: cut (J,I) and one edge that blocks H’s branch to I and to C/P simultaneously: edge (H, C) and (H, P) are two separate; cutting only one still leaves the other route to I via C–I or P–I. But if we cut (J,I) and cut (H, I), that leaves G–H–C–I and G–H–P–I as noted. To kill both with one edge, we could cut (H, C) and rely on (H, I) kept? No.

Conclusion: minimum is 3:
Choose to isolate I by removing three edges incident to it so that all paths break: remove (H,I), (J,I), and one of {(C,I), (P,I)} plus ensure G cannot reach the other? But G can reach both C and P via H, so we must cut both C–I and P–I if we don’t cut H–C or H–P.

A size-3 cut: remove (I,H), (I,J), (I,C). Then any remaining path would need I via P, but G can reach P through H, and P–I remains, giving G–H–P–I, so still connected. Hence need (I,P) instead of (I,C). Try (I,H), (I,J), (I,P): then G–H–C–I remains. So we must remove both (I,C) and (I,P) along with either (I,H) or (I,J) to break J branch: total 3? That is 3 edges: (I,C) and (I,P) and (I,J). Then any path from G must use H–I, but (I,H) remains, so G–H–I would still connect; so we must include (I,H) instead of one of them. To cut all, we need to remove at least three of the four edges around I. Any remaining single edge would provide a path from G to I because G can reach H, J, and through H to C and P. G can reach J directly via E/M/K routes; G can reach H directly; thus any single surviving incident edge to I will allow a path. Therefore the minimum cut is 3: remove any three of I’s four incident edges.

One valid minimal set: remove (I,H), (I,J), (I,C). Then check:
- Via H: blocked.
- Via J: blocked.
- Via C: blocked.
- Only remaining incident to I is (I,P). Can G reach P without using I? Yes, G–H–P exists. That would give G–H–P–I, but we left (I,P) intact; however H–P exists, so this would still connect. Hence this set fails. So the remaining single incident edge must be one that G cannot reach. But G can reach all four neighbors of I. Therefore any one remaining incident edge will allow a path.

Thus we must remove all four incident edges of I to ensure disconnection. Minimum cut is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(I, H), (I, J), (I, C), (I, P)]

Therefore, all G→I paths are of two types:
1) Through H–I, which necessarily uses edge (H, I).
2) Through F to E, which necessarily uses edge (F, E), then continue E→…→I (e.g., E–I does not exist, but E connects to B and M and L; from E you can go E–B–C–I, or E–M–J–I via P, etc.). Regardless, every such path must cross (F, E) first.

- Paths via H are broken because H no longer connects to I.
- Paths via F/X/K are broken at (F, E), isolating the entire G–{X,K,F} side from the rest of the graph that leads to I.

There is no edge from H to any other node that reaches I without using (H, I) directly; H’s other neighbor B would require G–H–B–… but to reach I from B you still need to go through C–I or to J/P which all lie beyond E/B region; however G–H–B path does not pass (F, E). That would create a path G–H–B–C–I that does not use (H, I) nor (F, E). Therefore my previous cut is insufficient.

We need to account for all paths from G through H→B→… to I.

From B to I options:
- B–C–I (edge C–I exists).
- B–H–I (uses H–I).
- B–E–…–I via other nodes.

So to block the H→B branch, we must cut either (H, B) or block at C–I.

   From G via H: direct H–I still exists unless we also cut (H, I). So (H, B) alone doesn’t stop G–H–I. We’d need (H, I) instead of (H, B).
Try set {(H, I), (F, E)}. Check G–H–B–C–I path: after removing (H, I), G–H–B–C–I still exists; it does not touch (F, E). So still connected.

From I to E, possible routes exist, but to reach H we would need (I, H), which we did NOT remove. Wait: (I, H) exists and remains, so if there is any E→I path, E could still reach H via I.

Check E→I paths:
- E–G–H–I would use (H, I), but we have removed (G, H), so E–G cannot go through H.
- E–B–C–O–P–I? Edges: B–C yes; C–O yes (diagonal); O–P yes (diagonal); P–I yes (horizontal). So E–B–C–O–P–I is a valid path to I, then I–H would reconnect to H, defeating the cut.
Therefore cutting only (G, H) and (C, H) is insufficient.

We must ensure all routes from E to I are cut or remove (I, H).

Try a different minimal strategy:
- Remove (I, H) to prevent any approach to H via I.
- Also break the direct G-route: either (E, G) or (G, H). If we remove (I, H) and (G, H), check remaining:
  - H still has neighbor C; path E–B–C–H would exist unless we also cut something on that route.
So with (I, H) removed, we must also block the C-route or G-route. If we cut (G, H) and (C, H), that’s two edges already and seems sufficient (since H would have no remaining neighbors). But H also has only three neighbors total (G, I, C). Removing the two edges (I, H) and (C, H) leaves G–H, permitting E–G–H. Similarly, removing (I, H) and (G, H) leaves C–H and allows E–B–C–H.

Conclusion: The only way to guarantee disconnection is to remove all three edges incident to H: (H, G), (H, I), and (H, C). This isolates H entirely. No smaller set of edges works because H has three disjoint entry points from the rest of the graph via G, I, and C, and there exist paths from E to each of G, I, and C.

Final Answer:

1) Through H: G–H–I is a direct 2-edge path. Blocking H alone cuts this route.
2) Through upper triangle to J/P: G can go to E then to M then to J; or G→F→J; or G→X→K→J; also G→F→P via J–P. From J or P we can reach I (J–I, P–I). - Removing H blocks the H path and also G→H→C because H is on those; but G can still reach C via E/B/N. So C path still exists unless C is removed.
- If we remove C together with H, the remaining routes must go through J or P. But without H and C, G can still reach J/P via E/F/X/K/M, so we must also cut J/P. Instead choose one node that hits both the J and P routes: removing J alone is insufficient due to P–I; removing P alone is insufficient due to J–I. We need one of {J, P} plus something else that eliminates the other connection.

- Paths via C are cut (C removed).

- G–H–I blocked (H removed).
- All routes using J to I blocked (J removed).

Therefore minimum cut requires removing both J and P plus either H or C?

8) O → C → E → M → outer-left frame → H

- The middle horizontal routes (via N/Y/C) funnel into B to reach H directly.
- Any use of the outer frames or the right-side F–X–K–G ladder to get to H requires going up from the center through E (via C→E or N→E or B→E), or from the lower side through P→J/outer, but then still needs the bottom/outer connection which leaves O’s neighborhood only through P.
- The bottom/left outer access from O goes uniquely through P.
Therefore, all O→H paths are covered by the three gateways B, E, and P.

Minimality:
- Removing only one node among {B, E, P} is insufficient:
  • Remove B: a path remains O → C → E → … → outer/right → H, or O → P → outer → H.
  • Remove E: a path remains O → N → B → H, or O → P → outer → H.
  • Remove P: a path remains O → N/Y/C → B → H, or O → C → E → … → H.
- Removing any two of them blocks all routes:
  • Remove B and E: the only remaining escape is via P to the outer frame, but H is reachable from O via the outer only if P exists; with P present but B and E gone, there is still a direct bottom outer link O→…→H through P. Hence we must check: the bottom outer from O indeed goes O→P→I→outer→H. If P remains, that path exists. Thus B+E alone is not sufficient.
  • Remove B and P: central horizontal to H is cut (B removed); outer/frames access is blocked because P is the only way from O to the outer/bottom; routes via E to the right/top ultimately need B (for the direct middle) or the outer (needs P). Hence no path remains.
  • Remove E and P: outer/frames are blocked at P; central middle via B is blocked because without E there is still O→N→B→H direct; wait, that path does not use E or P. So E+P is not sufficient.
Thus the effective minimal cut is size 2: {B, P}.

Verification:
- With B and P removed:
  • Paths using the middle horizontal are broken at B.
  • Paths using the outer frames or bottom route are broken at P (O cannot reach J/I/outer without P).
  • Paths attempting O → C → E → right/top cannot reach H because after E the only way to H not using outer is through the right vertical G→H. But from E to that region requires E→F/X→K→G; however O cannot reach E without going through C, which is fine, but from E→…→G→H would still be an O→E→… path bypassing B/P. Check connectivity: O→C→E→F→X→K→G→H is a continuous route that avoids B and P. Therefore we must ensure B,P removal stops this. It does not; that path exists if E remains.
Hence {B,P} is not sufficient.

Re-evaluate gateways: We must also cut the E→right ladder path.

Correct minimal cut:
- Consider {E, B}: With E and B removed:
  • Middle horizontal is cut at B.
  • Any attempt to go via right/top (E→F/X/K/G) is blocked at E.
  • Outer frames via top/left require E (from O to get onto top M/L or to reach right), while bottom/left outer via P still exists, giving O→P→I→outer→H. So E+B still allows bottom outer path unless P is also removed. Therefore need two nodes that jointly block middle and both outer/right ladders.

Try pairs involving E plus one node that blocks the bottom outer: that’s P.
- Remove E and P:

Try pairs involving B plus a node that blocks E→right routes: maybe G or F or K or X? But O could still reach outer via P and then to H without touching G/F/K/X. So we must also block P or block H’s access from outer. Blocking near H with G would cut the right vertical entry to H from top/outer; but bottom outer still connects directly to H along the outer frame on the right side, which enters H directly, not via G. Thus removing G does not block bottom outer.

Therefore, to disconnect all paths, we need three nodes: B (middle), E (bridge to right/top), and P (bottom/outer access). Check with {B, E, P}:
- Middle horizontal: cut at B.
- E→right/top ladder and top/left frame: cut at E.
- Bottom/outer access: cut at P.
- Any alternate? O→C→… without E can’t reach right ladder; without P it can’t reach bottom/outer; without B it can’t go straight to H. No remaining route from O to H.

No pair among nodes can simultaneously block all three independent route families, so the minimum is 3.

Connections:
- E connects to B, G, F, L, M. (Edges clearly drawn from E to these nodes.)
- H connects to G and to I and to B. (Edges H–G, H–I, H–B are visible.)

Thus every E→H path must reach one of H’s neighbors {G, I, B}.

Check disconnections by cutting minimal edges:

1) Via B: Paths like E–B–H or E–B–C–I–H etc. They all need edge (E, B) to leave E toward B, or edge (B, H) to reach H from B. Cutting (E, B) alone blocks all B-based routes because there’s no alternative edge from E to B’s side that arrives at B before H. But H could still be reached via G or I.

2) Via I: Any I-route from E goes E→…→J/I/C→H. A necessary final edge into H on these routes is (H, I). Removing (H, I) blocks all I-based routes regardless of how E reaches I.

3) Via G: Any G-route ends with (H, G). Removing (H, G) blocks all G-based routes.

Therefore, if we remove the two edges incident to H that are not shared with B, i.e., (H, I) and (H, G), there remains a possible path E–B–H through B. To block that as well, we must also remove either (E, B) or (B, H). Removing (E, B) is sufficient and does not affect other already blocked routes.

Verify minimality:
- With only one removed edge, at least two of the neighbor routes remain, giving a path.
- With two removed edges, at least one neighbor route remains (e.g., cut (H, I) and (H, G), path E–B–H still exists).
- With three edges removed as {(H, I), (H, G), (E, B)} there is no remaining route into H from E, since H’s only neighbors are G, I, B and all three approaches are blocked.

Hence the minimum cut size is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (H, G), (E, B)]

- Remove B to block the B branch.

Thus, to separate L from H, we must remove at least one node on each of the two disjoint exits from L: {E} and {M}-branch.
- Minimal choice: remove E and either M or F. Removing F blocks all M-branch paths because M’s only neighbor besides L is F.
- Verify: Remove {E, F}.
  - Paths via E are gone.
  - Paths via M are forced to go L→M, but M cannot proceed (F removed), so dead end.
  - No other edge leaves L, so H is disconnected from L.

Minimality:
- One node cannot suffice because L has two independent neighbors (E and M). With only one removal, the other neighbor still provides a path to the rest of the graph and to H.
- With two nodes, {E, F} or {E, M} both work and are minimal.

  - B connects to N, E, and C (a slanted edge to C), and also to F (slanted edge).
- Key observation: Every path from M to B must enter the “B neighborhood” through one of {E, N, C, F}.
  - From M via the outer right path, the first contact near B is E.
  - From M via P–O region, approaches B through N (P–O–N), through C (P–…–C), or through E (P–O–…–E), and the lower routes from M through I–H–… reach B only via C or F/E.
- Therefore, removing a single node among {E, N, C, F} is insufficient; other entries remain.
- Check if two nodes suffice:
  - If we remove E and N, remaining entries are C and F. Are there M→C or M→F paths that avoid E and N?
    - M→P exists. P connects to C directly (P–C via inner zigzag), and C connects to B directly. This would still connect unless C is removed.
    - Also, lower path M→…→H→…→F exists leading to B via F–B.
  - Hence we need to block both the “C-entry” and the “E/N-entry or F-entry”.
- Minimal separation is achieved by removing C and E:
  - Verify all paths:
    - B via N: Without E and with N present, can M reach N? From M to N requires going through O/P region then to N. But N connects to B; we must ensure M cannot reach N without passing E or C. From P one can go to O, then to N. However, O’s connections to the rest from M-side go through P; M→P→O→N would exist. But does that bypass E and C? Yes; then B would be reachable via N. Therefore removing C and E alone is not sufficient.
- Try removing N and C:
  - Entries left: E and F.
  - From M to E: outer right path M→…→E remains, giving M→E→B. So not sufficient.
- Try removing E and F:
  - Entries left: N and C.
  - From M to N: M→P→O→N gives path N→B, so still connected.
- Try removing N and F:
  - Entries left: E and C.
  - M→…→E→B still works. Not sufficient.
- Therefore we need three nodes to block all entries {E, N, C, F}. Removing any three that cover all entries will work.
- Choose a minimal cut: remove E, N, and C.
  - Check: With E, N, C removed, the only remaining neighbor into B is F. Can M reach F without using E/N/C?
    - Paths to F typically go from H/G/K/X side up to F; M can go down to P, I, H, etc. From H to F there is a direct edge. That would give M→…→H→F→B, but F→B edge exists. However we removed only E, N, C; F remains, so this path exists. So E,N,C is NOT sufficient.

Revise: We must remove F as part of the cut, meaning the cut must include F and also block E and N or C to avoid alternative entries.

- Consider removing F and E:
  - Entries left: N and C.
  - M can go M→P→O→N→B or M→…→C→B. So need to also remove either N or C. Therefore size 3 cut: {F, E, N} or {F, E, C}.
- Verify {F, E, N}:
  - Remaining entry is C.
  - Can M reach C when N and E are gone? Yes: M→P→(direct to C via inner edge). Then C→B is a direct edge, but C is still present, so that would connect. Hence {F, E, N} fails.

- Verify {F, E, C}:
  - Remaining entry is N.
  - Can M reach N with E and C removed? Path M→P→O→N still exists. Then N→B is direct. So {F, E, C} fails.

Thus even three among {E,N,C,F} may still leave a route unless that remaining one is unreachable from M due to removals upstream. But upstream removals are not among targets.

We need to instead find a two-node separator earlier:
- Note that many M→… routes funnel through P: M has only two immediate downward/side connections into the interior: to J and directly to P (vertical). From J you can go to D→P or J→P, so J-side also funnels into P. The long outer right path from M to E bypasses P entirely. Therefore, all “interior” routes go through P, while the “outer-right” route goes to E.

Therefore a minimal cut could be {P, E}:
- Remove P to block all interior routes from M to the network near B (via N, C, F), since J and D connect to P, and paths from M downward to I/H/C also rely on P? Check connections:
  - From M to lower-left (I/H) without P: The only visible connection from M to that region is via J→D→P→…; without P, J and D become dead ends. The direct long outer boundary from M goes to the right toward E; it does not connect to I/H without passing B-side. So removing P should sever M from O, N, C, Y, C, I/H/G/K/X region.
- Then remove E to cut the outer-right route M→…→E→B.
- Verify rigorously:
  - With P removed:
    - Edges to P: J–P, D–P, O–P, C–P are broken, isolating interior. M has no other edge downward except to J; J cannot reach anywhere toward B without P. The long slanted edge from M bypasses interior and reaches near E. So the only remaining M→B path would be through E.
  - With E removed:
    - The outer path from M cannot connect to B, since E is required on that right boundary and also E was the only neighbor from that outer path to reach the B line (E connects to L and to B and to F). Without E, the outer path ends at a dead node.
  - Check for any alternative M→B path not using P or E:
    - Could M connect to O directly? M connects to P only inwards; no direct M–O edge. 
    - Could there be M→…→N without P? N connects to O and Y and B; routes to N from M via interior all need P. No other connection from M to Y/O/C/I/H exists without P.
    - Could M reach F without P or E? Paths to F from the left go via H/G/K/X or via E; without P, M cannot reach H/G side; without E, the E→F edge also gone.
  - Therefore, all paths are blocked.

Minimality:
- Can a single node cut work? 
  - Removing E alone fails because M→P→O→N→B or M→P→C→B, etc.
  - Removing P alone fails because M can still go along the outer path to E→B.
  - Removing any other single node on the outer path (e.g., L) wouldn’t block the outer because M reaches E directly; and interior paths remain via P. So at least 2 nodes are necessary.
- Thus {P, E} is a valid minimum cut of size 2.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [P, E]

  6) O–B–E–F–…–G–H (through the right structure to G then to H)
  7) O–C–…–I–H (C also links to I by a long edge)
  8) Paths using Y: O–Y–C–H and O–Y–N–C–H, etc.

- To disconnect, cut at H’s incident edges is simplest. H has exactly three incident edges: (H, I), (H, C), (H, G). Removing all three isolates H from the rest of graph regardless of what happens around O.
- Verify necessity/minimality:
  - If only two are removed, there remains at least one path: 
    • If (H, C) remains, O–C–H connects them.
    • If (H, I) remains, O–P–I–H (or O–C–I–H) connects them.
    • If (H, G) remains, O–B–E–F–G–H (or O–C–G–H via K/X) connects them.
  - Therefore, all three must be removed; no smaller cut suffices.

Final Answer:

  1) G–H–I (direct via H)
  2) G–F–E–B–C–I
  3) G–F–E–B–C–O–P–I
  4) G–X–K–F–E–B–C–I (and variants through the K–X–F square)
  5) G–X–F–E–B–C–I
  6) G–H–B–C–I
  7) G–H–B–C–O–P–I
  8) G–H–P–I
  9) G–H–P–D–(back to P)–I is same as 8 since D is a dead end to P.
  10) Any detours through N/O/Y ultimately rejoin C/O/P before I, so they are covered by cutting at C↔I/P.

- Vertex H is a single articulation between the top middle and both G and I in several paths. Cutting (H, I) removes paths 1, 6, 7, 8.
- Remaining paths must go from G through the lower network via F/E/B/C to reach I. All of these require the edge (C, I) to enter I from below/top. Removing (C, I) breaks paths 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 that approach I via C.

Verification:
- After removing edges (H, I) and (C, I), there is no remaining edge into I from any node reachable from G: the only neighbors of I are H, P, and C. With (H, I) and (C, I) cut, the only neighbor left is P, but any G→…→P path would still need P–I to reach I. We must also consider P–I: path G–H–P–I would exist if (P, I) remains. However, since (H, I) is cut, G–H–P–I still uses P–I; but G can reach P via H (G–H–P). Thus we must cut (P, I) or disrupt reaching P from G. Cutting only (H, I) and (C, I) leaves G–H–P–I intact via edge (P, I). Therefore include (P, I).

- With (H, I) and (P, I) removed, the only remaining neighbor into I is C. Cutting (C, I) as selected removes all lower routes. No other edge into I remains, so G cannot reach I.

Minimality:
- At least two edges are necessary because G and I have three internally disjoint entrances to I: via H, via P, and via C. To block all, we must remove at least two if we could block access to two of these and also block G’s way to the third; but G can reach both H and the lower network independently. Therefore the simplest minimal cut is to remove all edges into I: (H, I), (P, I), and (C, I). Any set of only two leaves one entrance to I usable from G.
